Because the affective response is itself grounded in \textit{phantasia}, and because one and the same \textit{phantasia} retains what has elapsed (memory), presents what is at hand as already \textit{taken-as-good-or-bad} (\textit{aisthēsis}), and projects what is yet to come (anticipation), every \textit{pathos} is temporalized not by three faculties operating side by side but by the single capacity that holds the three together. Accordingly, \textit{phantasia} grounds not merely each ecstasis but their \textit{ecstatic unity}, the co-temporalizing of past, present, and future through which it is the soul's temporal medium.

Aristotle states in \textit{De Memoria} that we remember when the movement caused by the object is preserved internally (1, 450a20--25). This preserved internal movement, when the original object of preservation was itself an articulationally concrete emotion, is the \textit{resonant emotion}.\footnote{The temporal range Aristotle attributes to emotion is not a distinction between live and non-live emotion but a distinction in what \textit{triggers} a present-felt experience---present perception, future expectation, or memory re-engagement.}. To remember grief is to feel grief again; to anticipate pleasure is to feel pleasure \textit{now} in relation to a not-yet. As Aristotle states it in the \textit{Rhetorica}: 
\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
    [s]ome pleasant feeling is associated with most of our appetites; we are enjoying either the memory of a past pleasure or the expectation of a future one, just as persons down with fever, during their attack of thirst, enjoy remembering the drinks they have had and looking forward to having more. So also a lover enjoys talking or writing about his loved one, or doing any little thing connected with him; all these things recall him to memory and make him as it were present to the eye of imagination. (I.11, 1370b14--21)
\end{adjustwidth}
Because \textit{phantasia} generates appearances beyond the immediately present, the temporal depth of emotional life follows directly. Why? Because \textit{phantasia} retains, makes-present, and provides the \textit{phantasmata} from which future-projection is synthesized; \textit{phantasia} provides the \textit{phantasmata} which ground thought and which \textit{doxa}-ratifies, and that ratification is an inextricable, constitutive component that makes emotion what it is. Emotion is not anchored, as Aristotle makes abundantly clear in the passage above, but reaches into past and future by means of the \textit{phantasmatic} appearance. Fear, paradigmatically, is a imagined anticipation of a future evil felt in the present as pain or a disturbance. The feared event in and of itself has yet to come to pass, it does not exist, but the \textit{phantasma} does and the soul moves. \textit{Phantasia} is not, therefore, a decorative accompaniment to emotion; rather, it is the condition of possibility for the soul's being-moved by absences, possibilities, appearances, and counterfactuals---which is to say, the condition of possibility for the entire range of higher-order emotions placed at the center of evaluatively complex action.

Aristotle's per-emotion definitions distribute their primary temporal anchor while preserving traces of all three ecstases---and in fear each ecstasis Aristotle names is answered by a determination of Heidegger's own reading. As Heidegger observes, there are three temporal components to fear as defined by Aristotle: fear is ``a pain or disturbance due to imagining some destructive or painful evil in the future'' (\textit{Rhetorica} II.5, 1382a21). Heidegger categorizes Aristotle's imagined ``destructive or painful evil in the future'' as \gk{μέλλοντος} (\textit{méllontos}, ``what is to come''): as ``a \textit{disposition that is set before an approaching possibility that pertains to me, comes toward me, and as such announces itself, specifically through the announcement}'' (\textit{BCAP} 169). This futural component is not free-standing but is simultaneously grounded in the past (having been) and the present. ``\textit{Being afraid} must be characterized as an \gk{οἴεσθαι} [\textit{oíesthai}]. The one who is brought into fear must once ``believe'' that the definite thing that threatens, threatens \textit{him}, and further, that what is threatening proceeds \textit{from this definite human being,} and that he threatens him \textit{now}'' (\textit{BCAP} 173-4). The bodily ``pain or disturbance'' is felt \textit{now} as---what Heidegger refers to as---a \gk{ταραχή} [\textit{ταραχή}], ``disquiet'' that is itself temporally indexed: ``[t]he \textit{disquiet} is nothing other than the \textit{opposedness of \gk{οἴεσθαι} and \gk{ἐλπίς}}: believing oneself to be lost, and hoping nonetheless'' (\textit{BCAP} 174). The present \textit{disquiet} braids together a \textit{having-been} belief (\gk{οἴεσθαι}) that orients the `now' of fear with a \textit{futural} hope (\gk{μέλλοντος}) that serves as the \textit{orectic} element in the particular instance of fear: the desire to escape, flee, or avoid the imagined, impending ``dustructive or painful evil in the future.'' That dispositional belief (\gk{οἴεσθαι}) is the \textit{having-been}---``what is threatening is appropriated as mattering to one'' (\textit{BCAP} 174), the believing-oneself-vulnerable that being-there has disclosed---without which fear cannot exist at all: ``[i]t follows therefore that fear is felt by those who believe something to be likely to happen to them, at the hands of particular persons, in a particular form, and at a particular time'' (\textit{Rhetorica} II.5, 1382b33-1383a1).\footnote{Heidegger further underscores Aristotle's point in his differentating between feeling threatened and feeling fear: ``Where this distinctive \gk{οἴεσθαι} is wanting, one can perhaps be aware of a threatening thing, but it cannot be feared. This being-for remains absent from those for whom it goes swimmingly, who regard themselves as safe from every threat, those who have riches, physical strength, followers and influence at their disposal. These are never brought into fear; with them there arises a distinctive disdain, arrogance, and impudence'' (\textit{BCAP} 174; Heidegger seems to be referencing \textit{Rhetorica} II.5, 1383a1--7).} The \textit{having-been} belief of vulnerability, \textit{present} disquiet, and \textit{futural} desire to flee are not separate stages or layers, but are experienced simultaneously as the co-temporalization of the three ecstases. 

Anger is ``a desire accompanied by pain, for a conspicuous revenge for a conspicuous slight'' (II.2, 1378a31--32): the slight has already occurred (\textit{having-been}) orienting the pain and pleasure felt now (\textit{present}) for an anticipated revenge (\textit{futural}). Indeed, Aristotle remarks, citing Homer, that the anticipation of revenge is sweeter ``by far than honeycomb dripping with sweetness'' (II.5, 1378b7; citing \textit{Iliad} XVIII 109). Pity is ``pain at an apparent evil, destructive or painful, which befalls one who does not deserve it, and which we might expect to befall ourselves or some friend of ours, and moreover to befall us soon'' (II.8, 1385b13--16): the witnessing is present, the production of suffering reaches back to its past and orients the present, and the \textit{phantasmatic} projection---``might befall ourselves'' or our friends and soon---opens the futural projection through which the witness is implicated in what is witnessed. Lastly, Aristotle's definition of shame makes the co-temporalizing structure of the three ecstases explicit: ``Shame may be defined as pain or disturbance in regard to bad things, whether \textit{present}, \textit{past}, or \textit{future}, which seem likely to involve us in discredit'' (II.6, 1383b15--16). Aristotle's own formulation names all three ecstases explicitly; regardless of the temporally indexing for the cause of the shame (past, present, or future), the pain is felt in the present, conditioned by the past, and projected onto the future. What Heidegger's reading adds to Aristotle's distribution is the \textit{hexis}-correlate of the \textit{having-been}: the dispositional ground into which being-there has already settled is what makes \textit{courage} possible as the cultivated capacity to fear well. ``Fear has, as a determinate \gk{πάθος} [\textit{pathos}], the possibility of a \gk{ἕξις} [\textit{hexis}]. Such a possibility is \textit{courage}.... \textit{Fear is the condition of the possibility of courage}'' (\textit{BCAP} 175). Thus, Heidegger's threefold \gk{οἴεσθαι} (\textit{having-been-dispositioned}), bodily felt \gk{ταραχή} (\textit{present-disquiet}), and \gk{μέλλοντος} (\textit{futural-projection}) are derived from Aristotle's own analytic, recovered as the ecstatic temporal articulation of affect itself.\footnote{Indeed, Heidegger anchors his temporal analysis of fear in Aristotle's own definition and cites him directly: ``Aristotle rightly defines ``fear'' as \gk{λύπη τις ἢ ταραχή}---as ``a kind of depression or bewilderment''.'' (\textit{BT} 392).} 

\textit{Being and Time} makes the framework general: ``[j]ust as understanding is made possible primarily by the future, and moods are made possible by having been, the third constitutive item in the structure of care---namely, \textit{falling}---has its existential meaning in the \textit{Present}'' (\textit{BT} 396-397). Each existential structure has its primary ecstasis: understanding is primarily futural, disposedness (\textit{Befindlichkeit}) primarily grounded in having-been, falling primarily in the present. Yet each ecstasis co-temporalizes with the other two, fear paradigmatically so. ``The specific ecstatical unity which makes it existentially possible to be afraid, temporalizes itself primarily ... as a mode of having been,'' which ``modifies its Present and its future in their own temporalizing'' (392). The phenomenon of fear, accordingly, is constituted not by a single tense but by the ecstatic unity of all three. The point generalizes to every \textit{pathos}: each is a determinate configuration of how the three ecstases co-temporalize in unity, with a primary ecstasis that gives the affect its distinctive phenomenal character, and the other two ecstases co-active as the ground (having-been) and the projection (futural) that sustain the present feeling-itself of the affect. 

The Aristotelian lectures formulate the principle in its most concentrated form---and they state it of \textit{concern} and \textit{concernful speaking} together: [b]eing in itself as concern and concernful speaking is \textit{temporal}, concerns the \textit{not-yet-present}, speaks about what \textit{has-happened-already}, treats the \textit{existing-there-right-now}'' (\textit{BCAP} 131). The emotions stand on both sides of this formulation at once: as modes of disposedness (\textit{Befindlichkeit}) they attune the concern it names, and as the ground out of which speaking arises they are what lends concernful speech its tense. The threefold span the passage predicates of concern and \textit{logos} is  not one the emotions borrow from elsewhere; it is the span they already articulate, and which concern and speech inherit from them. The temporal manifold is, accordingly, no external imposition on the emotions but their own internal articulation raised to explicitness in the speaking it grounds. The primary ecstasis is what gives the affect its name and phenomenal character, while the co-active ecstases supply the conditions of possibility that hold the affect in being.

\subsection{Emotion's Function in the Chain} 

The coordinated notation for the actualization chain has now been settled: nodes $A_0$ through $A_4$ for the major actualization stages, with $A_3$ as the completed cognitive actuality at which \textit{doxa} ratifies the evaluative content; and transitions $M_n \rightarrow A_{n+1}$ for the motions between nodes, with $M_3 \rightarrow A_4$ designating the motion that emotion \textit{is}. Aristotle is quite clear on this: ``being pained or pleased, being bold or fearful, being angry, perceiving, thinking. All these are regarded as modes of movement'' not of the soul but movements originating or terminating in the soul (\textit{DA} I.4, 408b1--18). Therefore, if emotions are a movement it must participate in the three-factor schema Aristotle lays out: unmoved mover, moved mover, and that which is moved. The unmoved mover of animal action is always the object of desire and real or apparent good. The world being disclosed as mattering in some way as an object of desire: an object to pursue or to avoid, for it is only the object of desire ``that itself remaining unmoved originates the movement by being apprehended in thought or imagination'' (III.10, 433b11-13). For emotion, the apparent good is the \textit{toward-which} that is \textit{doxastically}-ratified. If we consider anger, the apparent good is the anticipated  revenge articulated resulting from \textit{doxa}'s ratification that an undeserved slight has occurred. The slight \textit{discloses} revenge-as-good-and-pursuable and it is \textit{doxa}'s ratification that articulates the situation such that this particular good is revealed. The moved mover remains the \textit{orektikon}: ``that which at once moves and is moved is the faculty of appetite [\textit{orexis}] (for that which is moved is moved insofar as it desires [\textit{orexis}], and appetite [\textit{orexis}] in the sense of actual appetite [\textit{orexis}] is a kind of movement)'' (III.10, 433b17-19). \textit{Orexis} remains the moved mover, and the cognitive \textit{doxastic} evaluation only \textit{determines which form the desire takes}. Emotion is the actualization of the \textit{orektikon} into a specific form of desire. Emotion, then, \textit{is} the moved mover in its \textit{doxa}-articulated actualization: a form of \textit{orexis} specific to the cognitive evaluation of the object. Lastly, that which is moved is the body and the somatic preparation of the ``organic parts'' is the \textit{joint}. Through enmattered affection, emotion prepares the organic parts by heating or chilling. This affection is the boundary where the moved mover terminates in the moved; it is not a separate step but \textit{how the moved mover moves the moved at all}. 


Emotion, accordingly, is not a node in the chain but the \textit{form} that desire takes within it. Depending upon \textit{doxa}-ratification at $A_3$ for its cognitive-evaluative component, emotion is the determinate form \textit{orexis} takes in the $M_3 \rightarrow A_4$ motion---the form of desire that at once prepares the somatic parts and orients the animal toward a specific action. Fear is the exemplary case: the \textit{doxa} that affirms an anticipated evil \textit{as} destructive-for-me is the condition under which \textit{orexis} takes its determinate form as the desire-to-flee---a conative element individuated by just that evaluative content and structural to the fear, with the chilling of the blood that readies the body and the flight toward which that readied body is already turned. What the chain requires at $M_3 \rightarrow A_4$, under evaluatively complex conditions, is the concretization of \textit{orexis} into a determinate form of desire directed at a specific object under a specific evaluative description. Emotion is precisely this concretization. Without it, the chain would have a generic moved mover---\textit{orexis} set in motion by the apparent good---but no mechanism to specify \textit{which} form of desire corresponds to \textit{which} disclosure of the apparent good. The desire to flee is not the desire to retaliate; the desire to relieve is not the desire to conceal. Each determinate conative orientation is individuated by the cognitive evaluative structure of the emotion that specifies it, and each draws upon a determinate pattern of somatic preparation---the cooling of fear, the heating of anger, the sympathetic pain of pity---that configures the body for the action the emotion calls for.

Emotion does not precede desire as a separate antecedent stage; emotion \textit{is} the form desire takes when \textit{doxa} has disclosed evaluatively complex content. Fear is not something that happens before the desire to flee; fear \textit{is} the desire to flee insofar as it is structured by the cognitive evaluation of an anticipated destructive evil, experienced in the somatic alteration of chilled blood, and accompanied by the presently felt disturbance at the future evil and the pleasure of prospective escape. Anger is not something that happens before the desire to retaliate; anger \textit{is} the desire to retaliate insofar as it is structured by the perception of an unjustified slight, experienced in the somatic alteration of heated blood, and accompanied by the presently felt pain of the slight with the pleasure at the thought of anticipated revenge. The \textit{Rhetorica}'s definitions confirm this composite structure: each emotion is a desire of a certain kind, attended by pain or pleasure, directed at a certain kind of object, under a certain evaluative description. The composite is not several parallel components stacked together but a single articulated determination in which all are mutually constitutive aspects of one and the same \textit{emotion-as-being-affected}. 

The three-factor schema of \textit{De Anima} III.10 (433a9-b30) is preserved without revision. What the schema's original formulation leaves unspecified is the \textit{internal} complexity the moved mover acquires when the apparent good is articulated under the evaluative categories of the \textit{Rhetorica}---when the slighter, the threat, the undeserved sufferer, the public falling-short have entered the soul through \textit{doxa} as evaluatively determinate disclosures of the apparent good. Emotion names this internal complexity. It is the actualization of the \textit{orektikon} into a specific form of desire directed at a specific object under a specific evaluative description; it is the form in which the movement that originates in the soul becomes the body's being-moved; it is the mechanism through which the world's disclosure as mattering in a certain way becomes the animal's motion in the corresponding direction. 

The causal function emotion supplies is therefore not universal across the chain but specific to the evaluatively complex case. Where the apparent good is straightforwardly pleasant or painful and the desire is basic \textit{epithymia} or aversion---the drinking case of \textit{De Motu Animalium} (701a32-33)---basic hedonic tonality alone suffices to direct the organism toward or away from the object. At $M_3 \rightarrow A_4$ in the \textit{epithymetic} case, the chain runs without internal complexity: \textit{orexis} is generic, and the body's being-moved is the immediate consequence of the perception's hedonic tonality. The concretization of emotion is reserved for the cases in which \textit{doxa} has done its articulational work. 

\subsection{Conclusion}

Having worked through both Aristotle's analyses and Heidegger's reading of them, the section's central claim can now be stated in its most concentrated form. Each \textit{pathos}---taking \textit{pathos} as the all-encompassing term for being-affected in the broadest sense described in the \textit{Metaphysica}---is a determinate concretion of one ontological structure: the structure articulated by the four senses of the \textit{Metaphysica}, the three constitutive aspects of the \textit{Rhetorica}, the enmattered account of the \textit{De Anima}, and the ecstatic temporality that \textit{phantasia} holds together. That structure covers the full register that runs from \textit{epithymia} (hedonic tonality in which the object of \textit{orexis} is actively present to the perceiving animal, as water is to the thirsty), through \textit{resonant epithymia} (hedonic tonality in which the object is not present and \textit{phantasia} is operating, the \textit{phantasma} substituting for the perceived object),\footnote{It bears reiterating that the \textit{orectic} side of hedonic tonality---\textit{epithymia} when the object is present, \textit{resonant epithymia} when the object is absent and \textit{phantasia} operates on the \textit{resonant} \textit{kinēsis} to present the \textit{phantasma}---is co-given throughout with the perceptual side: the \textit{aisthēma} (the perceptual disclosure of the object's form) and \textit{resonant aisthēma} (its persisting \textit{eidetic} tone carried by the \textit{phantasma}) are one substrate but different in being with the \textit{orectic} side (\textit{De Anima} III.7, 431a13--14). The two sides cannot be separated; wherever the perceptual disclosure occurs, the \textit{orectic} aspect is co-given, and vice versa. Each \textit{pathos} actualization is, therefore, always already a co-given disclosure of perceptual and \textit{orectic} content, whether at the minimal articulation of pure \textit{epithymetic} \textit{aisthēsis} or the maximal articulation of the higher-order emotions.} to the most articulationally concrete emotions of the \textit{Rhetorica}. The differences across this register are not differences of ontological structure but of \textit{articulational concretion}: the structure is filled maximally in the emotions and minimally---but never absently---in \textit{aisthēsis}. The section's central thesis is, therefore, not a phenomenology proprietary to the \textit{Rhetorica}'s catalog but an ontology of being-affected as such, of which the catalog is the case disclosed at the most maximal level of articulation.\footnote{Gross's \textit{Uncomfortable Situations} develops a parallel social-ontological reading of emotion: ``the tradition after Aristotle's \textit{Rhetorica} offers alternatives for understanding emotions as social phenomena, which is something that leading humanists like Martha Nussbaum and Richard Sorabji should have remembered as they rushed toward the latest brain science'' (3). And: ``[r]hetoric, as a humanistic form of affordance theory, posits a situation that is not neutral but is rather `persuasive,' threatening and promising with respect to our human being-in-the-world'' (20). The structure developed here---integrating the ontology of alteration, the constitutive aspects of \textit{pathos}, the form-material unity, and the ecstatic temporality---gives the structural-ontological architecture that Gross's transactional-rhetorical reading articulates at the contemporary register; both reject the inner-state model of emotion in favor of a being-in-the-world account.}

This one structure integrates its four articulations without reducing to any. The \textit{Metaphysica}'s fourfold supplies the underlying ontology of alteration---the bedrock in virtue of which \textit{pathos} is the kind of being-affected it is, and from which the \textit{Rhetorica}'s generic ``change'' inherits its content; the \textit{Rhetorica}'s threefold gives the constitutive aspects of any \textit{pathos}---the change-in-disposition, the \textit{krisis} or position-taking that \textit{doxa} ratifies, and the hedonic tonality co-given with both; and, lastly, the ecstatic temporality that \textit{phantasia} holds together gives the threefold span of \textit{having-been}, \textit{present}, and \textit{projected-futural} across which every \textit{pathos} articulates being-there. The decisive addition is the enmattered account of the \textit{De Anima} that secures the structure's reach across the whole register: because every \textit{pathos} is bodily-in-the-world, the dryness of thirst is as constitutive of its \textit{pathos} as the boiling of blood is of anger, and no affection---from the barest hedonic tonality to the most articulate emotion---occurs except as an affection of the body. The inseparability of \textit{eidos} from \textit{hylē} is the condition on which the whole register is one structure rather than two adjacent kinds. 

The structure of emotion has its philosophical center of gravity, accordingly, in the claim that the emotions are the ground of \textit{logos}:
\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
The \gk{πάθη} (\textit{pathē}) are not merely an annex of psychical processes, but are rather \textit{the ground out of which speaking arises, and which what is expressed grows back into}; the \gk{πάθη}, for their part, are \textit{the basic possibilities in which being-there itself is primarily oriented toward itself}, finds itself. The primary being-oriented, the illumination of its being-in-the-world is not a \textit{knowing}, but rather a \textit{finding-oneself} that can be determined differently, according to the mode of being-there of a being. (\textit{BCAP} 176)
\end{adjustwidth}
Speaking, on this reading, is not a master operation that draws on the emotions as resources; speaking arises out of the emotions and returns to them, the feedback structure of which the \textit{pathetic} loop analysis above has specified at the \textit{pathos} level itself. The rhetorical art, accordingly, is the cultivation of the dispositional ground that brings being-there to discourse, and Aristotle's \textit{Rhetorica} is, therefore, not a manual of persuasion-techniques but a phenomenology of the disclosive structures through which being-there finds itself as being-with-others.\footnote{Kisiel's reading of supplies the matched political and philosophical articulation: ``\textit{care} is deeply ensconced in the life of pathos, mood. The cultivation of the appropriate mood of the auditor by the skilled speaker, so that the attentive listener will then speak with the speaker by speaking after him or her (\textit{Nachreden}) in contagious attunement, suggests that speech finds its deepest roots in mood ([GA] 177). Discourse is not only rational, but impassioned, or better, passional in its rationality \ldots\ As Heidegger puts it, `\textit{ethos} and \textit{pathos} are constitutive of \textit{legein} itself' ([GA] 165)'' (Kisiel 145).} What Heidegger's recovery discloses is that the emotions are not psychological phenomena to be added to a prior account of cognition; they are the prior. They are the modes in which being-there has its world disclosed to it, and on the basis of which both cognition and speaking become possible. This is why position-taking runs the whole register while speech does not: every \textit{pathos} carries a \textit{krisis}---even the thirsty animal discriminates \textit{this-as-drink}---but only the higher-order emotions carry that position-taking into \textit{logos}, into the speech-with-another before whom one is angered, ashamed, or moved to pity. To analyze anger, fear, shame, pity, indignation, kindness---the family of higher-order emotions Aristotle catalogs---is to undertake the phenomenology of disclosedness itself: the inquiry into how a finite, embodied, speaking being finds itself in the world before any explicit knowing or saying, and on the basis of which knowing and saying become possible at all. Heidegger's retrospective citation of the \textit{Rhetorica} as ``the first systematic hermeneutic of the everydayness of Being-with-one-another'' (\textit{BT} 178) is, in this light, not a passing historical note but a precise diagnostic: the \textit{Rhetorica}'s catalog of emotions is the first sustained phenomenology of attunement (\textit{Stimmung}), and the Aristotelian lectures are the preparatory work in which that phenomenology is recovered for the disposedness (\textit{Befindlichkeit}) of \textit{Being and Time}.

The disclosive role that the emotions discharge in the actualization of desire (perception-to-action) chain depends on a feature named at several points above but that warrants explicit final statement: emotion responds to appearance, not to corrected judgment. If \textit{phantasia} supplies the appearance, \textit{doxa} is the moment of the soul's yes-saying to propositional content, the unfolding from \textit{taking-as} to \textit{taking-as-true}; the emotion that follows is a being-moved keyed to the appearance, not to whatever rational correction the agent may apply later on. This is precisely what makes the chain run with the speed and inevitability that Aristotle ascribes to it (\textit{De Anima} III.3, 427b21--24). For example, the sound mistaken for a rattlesnake produces real fear, even though the corrected judgment will dissolve the mistake and diffuse if not eliminate the fear elicited; false beliefs generate real emotions because emotion is keyed to the \textit{phantasmatic} appearance, not to its eventual correction. Art produces emotion, because the \textit{phantasmatic} representation suffices to occasion the affective movement, even when the audience knows the represented event is unreal; and, emotion can contradict one's standing judgment, as when the knowledge that the coiled rope in the corner is not a snake does not stop the muted fear from rising because it still \textit{appears} snake-like.

The practical, world-shaping power of emotion that Aristotle's rhetorical theory makes explicit follows from this analysis. To persuade is not primarily to offer proofs but to reshape the listener's \textit{phantasmatic} field. The rhetor must supply images---concrete, narrative, affectively charged---that allow the audience to see what the rhetor wants them to see, since this imagistic seeing grounds the subsequent formation of \textit{doxa}, the ensuing movement of emotion, and the eventual act of judgment. If rhetorical persuasion succeeds, accordingly, it is because it sets off the entire cascade of movement: new \textit{phantasma}, new \textit{doxa}, new emotion, new orientation, new action. For Aristotle, in sum, emotion is the kinetic, affective, evaluative dimension of how the world appears to a finite, embodied, speaking being. It is grounded in \textit{phantasia}, shaped through involuntary \textit{doxa}, felt as pleasure or pain, prepared somatically, and expressed as motion toward or away from what the soul takes to be significant. Emotion is not an add-on to rationality, nor a disruption of cognition, nor a mere physiological disturbance, but the way the soul's self-movement discloses significance at all. The single ontological structure---formal-material and ecstatic-temporal, articulated through the fourfold of the \textit{Metaphysica}, the threefold of the \textit{Rhetorica}, and the enmattered account of the \textit{De Anima}---specifies the architecture of this disclosure for each of the determinate emotions the \textit{Rhetorica} catalogs. Emotion, in its full Aristotelian sense, is nothing less than the affective architecture of \textit{bodily-being-in-the-world-with-others}.

\section{\texorpdfstring{$A_4$}{A4}: The Seven Causes of Action and the Affective Architecture of Being-in-the-World}

\inlinenote{``Now every action of every person either is or is not due to that person himself. Of those not due to himself some are due to chance, the others to necessity; of these latter, again, some are due to compulsion, the others to nature. Consequently all actions that are not due to a man himself are due either to chance or to nature or to compulsion. All actions that \textit{are} due to a man himnself and caused by himself are due either to habit or to desire; and of the latter, some are due to rational desire, the others to irrational. Rational desire is wishing, and wishing is a desire for good---nobody wishes for anything unless he thinks it good. Irrational desire is twofold, viz. anger and appetite'' (\textit{Rhetorica} I.10, 1368b33--1369a4). 
``Thus every action must be due to one or other of seven causes: change, nature, compulsion, habit, reasoning, anger or appetite'' (\textit{Rhetorica} I.10, 1369a5--6).

``Appetite is the cause of all actions that appear pleasant. Things familiar and things habitual belong to the class of pleasant things; for there arae many actions not naturally pleasant which men perform with pleasure, once they have become used to them. To sum up then, all actions due to ourselves either are or seem to be either good or pleasant. Moreover, as all actions due to ourselves are done voluntarily and actions not due to ourselves are done involuntarily, it follows that all voluntary actions must either be or seem to be either good or pleasant; for I reckon among goods escape from evils or apparent evils and the exchange of a greater evil for a less (since these things are in a sense desirable), and likewise I count among pleasures escape from painful or apparently painful things and the exchange of a greater pain for a less'' (\textit{Rhetorica} I.10, 1369a16--29). 
}
\inlinenote{Jakob Von Uexküll's concept of \textit{Umwelt} offers a structurally productive analogue for the \textit{resonant kinēsis}'s ongoing function. The \textit{Umwelt} is not a passive backdrop against which perception occurs but a meaningful environment that the organism constitutes through its perceptual and effectual organs (von Uexküll, \textit{A Foray Into the Worlds of Animals and Humans}, pp.~222--224). The \textit{resonant kinēsis} can thus be understood as a modification of the organism's \textit{Umwelt}: the persisting impression does not merely linger in the organ as a static trace but actively shapes the organism's subsequent encounters with its environment, determining what it notices, what it ignores, and how it responds. Von Uexküll's framework is biological and anti-anthropocentric; the present analysis restricts the analogy to the basic-valence level, where it holds equally for rational and non-rational animals---the persisting trace shapes the perceptual world the animal inhabits, whether or not that animal is capable of the higher-order operations \textit{phantasia} and \textit{doxa} will enable.\footnote{Gross's \textit{Uncomfortable Situations} develops a contemporary articulation of the \textit{Umwelt}/affordance framework in a directly relevant register: the human environment is constituted by ``negative'' and ``hostile'' affordances (Gross's signature extension of Gibson) that emerge precisely because the human \textit{Umwelt} is suffused with linguistic and rhetorical meaning. As Gross puts it: ``Rhetoric, as a humanistic form of affordance theory, posits a situation that is not neutral but is rather `persuasive,' threatening and promising with respect to our human being-in-the-world'' (Gross 20). The extension to rational-animals can be located at the site where rhetorical-historical situatedness converts the bare biological \textit{Umwelt} into a rhetorical-affordance environment. Thomas Rickert's \textit{Ambient Rhetoric} recovers the pre-Socratic and Platonic \textit{periechon}---``that which surrounds, encompasses, or makes room for''---which appears alongside \textit{chora} in Plato's \textit{Timaeus} and was already in play with Anaximenes, Hippocrates, and Empedocles, indexing an awareness that the encompassing environment is itself alive and that it constitutes the human condition both materially and spiritually (Rickert 6--7). Rickert's recovery culminates in the claim that taking rhetoric as ambient amounts to ``a renewed sensibility to what the ancient Greeks called \textit{periechon}'' (Rickert 285). \textit{Umwelt} as modern-biological and \textit{periechon} as ancient-Greek framings together extend the structurally productive analogue from the Aristotelian medium-doctrine through contemporary affordance theory. Heidegger directly engages Uexküll in \textit{The Fundamental Concepts of Metaphysics} (GA 29/30, §§42--58), reading Uexküll's \textit{Funktionskreis} model into the worldhood-analysis and distinguishing animal captivation (\textit{Benommenheit}) from human world-formation (\textit{Weltbildung}). This distinction handles the extension of the \textit{Umwelt}-analogy to rational animals: at the basic hedonic-tonality stage of the chain, the perceptual world the animal inhabits is structured by Umwelt-relations whether or not the animal is capable of \textit{doxa}-mediated articulation; the distinction between captivation and world-formation marks the divide at which higher-order rational operations transform the bare Umwelt-structure.}}

\inlinenote{``In the case of an \textit{action}---in the narrow sense in which it is opposed to \gk{ποίησις} [\textit{poiēsis }, ``making'']---it does not, according to its sense, depend on the action simply ending, on a result coming about; instead, \gk{προαίρεσις} [\textit{prohairesis}, ``deliberate choice''] is decisive, the manner and mode of ``resolving oneself.'' It belongs to action that it arise in each case out of a \textit{resolution}. Action itself has its \gk{τέλος} [\textit{telos}] in the \gk{καιρός} [\textit{kairos}, ``opportune moment'']. Therefore, it belongs to action that it \textit{proceed by way of deliberating and as such be fulfilled}. In \gk{ὀρθότης} [\textit{orthotes}], the action is fulfilled in the ``correctness'' of deliberation. With training, the possibility of action is put out of play, deliberating and resolving, the how of action---precisely that on which it depends. Therefore, to be able to be brough into the possibility of right action does not mean to appropriate a completedness. The manner and mode of habituation, in the case of action, is not practice but \textit{repetition}. Repetition does not mean the bringing-into-play of a settled completedness, but rather \textit{acting anew in every moment on the basis of the corresponding resolution}'' (\textit{BCAP} 127-128).}


%---BEGIN 01-A4 (reworked 2026-06-13)---
\subsubsection{$A_4$ in the Chain: Completed Action and the Three Structures of Its Analysis}

\inlinenote{Aristotle's \textit{hexis}: ``The frequently is, precisely, that which characterizes the temporality of being-there. Aristotle cannot say \gk{ἀεί} (\textit{aei}) insofar as human being-there does not so comport itself constantly and always. It can constantly be otherwise. The \textit{always} of a being like being-there is the \textit{frequently of repetition}. It is the being-there of human beings, as determined by \textit{historicality}, to see entirely different time-contexts in relation to which the remaining time-determinations break down'' (129). provides a temporal framework: memory is a mode of \textit{Gewesenheit}, a living dimension of \textit{Dasein}'s being that continues to determine present understanding and future projection. The having-been is not a dead past but a living orientation that conditions the soul's current cognitive engagement.}

Completed action stands as the terminal actuality of the synchronic chain---the moment at which a perceiving, desiring, and deliberating soul has converted its grasp of the realizable good into enacted bodily motion. Such action is not, however, of a single kind, nor is action the chain's only terminus. The chain may come to its end in three ways: in either of two kinds of action, which may also combine in one deed, or in a third terminus that lies beyond action altogether. The first kind is \textit{poiēsis}---making, production---the action whose \textit{telos} lies beyond the doing, in a product (\textit{ergon}) that outlasts it; it is the actualization that \textit{technē} governs, the craft-knowledge perfected in bringing such results reliably about (\textit{NE}, VI.4, 1140a1--10). The second is \textit{praxis}---action, doing in the strict sense---the action whose \textit{telos} is immanent in the doing itself, so that in acting well one has, in the same stroke, acted well; it is governed not by a craft but by an ethical \textit{hexis}, the cultivated \textit{how} of one's comportment toward the world---a manner of being, not a technique for producing (\textit{NE}, VI.5, 1140b6--7). The contrast between these two is, at bottom, one between two kinds of actualization: \textit{poiēsis} is a \textit{kinēsis}, complete only when its product stands forth, whereas \textit{praxis} is an \textit{energeia}, whole at every moment of its exercise (\textit{Metaphysics} IX.6, 1048b18--34). The third terminus is no action at all but the highest \textit{energeia} of \textit{nous}---\textit{theōria}, contemplation, which seeks no end beyond itself and in which the chain's \textit{nous}-route comes to rest in its object rather than issuing in any deed (\textit{NE} X.7, 1177b17--26). Because it issues in no deed and engages no dispositional ground of conduct, \textit{theōria} is set aside in what follows---whose concern is action and the \textit{hexis} from which it springs---and is taken up where \textit{phantasia} and \textit{nous} are developed. A completed action proper, for its part, may be at once a making, in respect of the product it leaves behind, and a doing, in respect of the comportment it enacts---and the bivalence of \textit{hexis} this implies, dividing the disposition that contracts deliberation toward routine from the one that holds the agent open to the \textit{kairos},\footnote{\textit{Kairos} (\gk{καιρός}) is the opportune or decisive moment---the qualitatively right time for action, as against \textit{chronos}, time as measured duration. A \textit{kairos}-responsive disposition is cultivated not to apply a fixed rule but to meet the singular demand of the situation as it arises---the readiness Heidegger glosses as ``holding-oneself-open'' rather than routine (\textit{BCAP}, p.~128).} is what the analysis below draws out in full.

Beneath both kinds of action lies a more rudimentary register the chain must also accommodate: the bare animal action shared by everything that perceives. Here a creature perceives something and responds at once---startling from a shadow, recoiling from what springs toward it---with no deliberation, no taking-as-true, no cultivated disposition brought to bear. In the very act of perceiving, \textit{aisthēsis} discriminates what it meets as to-be-pursued or to-be-shunned (\textit{DA} III.7, 431a8--12); sensitive \textit{phantasia} attaches the answering tone, attractive or aversive; and the simplest \textit{orexis}, \textit{epithymia}, reaches or recoils accordingly. Such movement is formally a doing and not a making, and so stands on the side of \textit{praxis}; but it is not yet the \textit{praxis} of an ethical \textit{hexis}, for it engages neither \textit{doxa} nor any cultivated \textit{how} of comportment. It is the floor of action---the limit at which perception and bare desire alone suffice to move the animal---the case every richer action presupposes.

Whichever way it ends---in a work made or a deed done---$A_4$ is the gathering-point at which the whole chain converges, and three structures, nested one within another, organize that convergence and supply the means of analyzing it. The first is the three-factor schema of motion: every animal movement involves an unmoved originator, a moved mover, and a moved (\textit{DA} III.10, 433b13--18). The second is the doctrine of the four causes, the explanatory whys by which to know a thing is to know its cause (\textit{Phys}. II.3). The third is the \textit{Rhetorica}'s sorting of all human action into seven causes, divided by whether the action springs from the agent or not (\textit{Rhet}. I.10, 1368b33--1369a6). These are not three rival accounts but three lenses of widening grain: the three factors give the bare anatomy of self-movement; the four causes set that anatomy within the general form of explanation; the seven causes individuate, for human action, which source moves the agent and whether the motion is the agent's own. Together they furnish at once the ontology of completed action---what it is for the soul to move itself to an end---and the method by which any discrete action may be diagnosed. The rest of this section sets out the three in turn and then sets them to work on a single case.

%---END 01-A4---

%---BEGIN ThreeStructures (2026-06-13; replaces 02-Phantasia / 03-ThreeFactor / 04-SevenCauses / 05-DoxRatification)---
\subsubsection{The Three Structures of Action: Ontology and Method}

Aristotle's analysis of animal movement turns on three factors: that which originates the movement without being moved, the realizable good; that which both moves and is moved, the faculty of desire; and that which is moved, the body (\textit{DA} III.10, 433b13--18). Desire is thus the source of all animal action whatever, the moved mover through which every motion of the living body passes; at its most basic it is \textit{epithymia}, the bare pursuit of the good and avoidance of the bad that the discriminating dimension of \textit{aisthēsis} institutes in the very act of perceiving (\textit{DA} III.7, 431a8--12), and which every perceiving animal exercises.\footnote{Throughout, \textit{epithymia} is used in this broad register---the elementary desiderative pursuit-and-avoidance shared by all animals with sense---which includes but exceeds the narrower bodily appetite the \textit{Rhetorica} names among the seven causes. In the broad sense it is the floor of animal desire as such; in the narrow sense, one source of human action among several.} What varies is not the schema but the point of entry---the mode in which the realizable good is made present to the animal so as to set desire in motion. Aristotle names three: ``I want to drink, says appetite; this is drink, says sense or imagination or thought: straightaway I drink'' (\textit{MA} 7, 701a32--33). Sense, imagination, and thought are not three chains but three doors onto one chain. And because each is a making-present of the object to the soul, \textit{phantasia} is essential to all three: it is the kinetic medium in which the object is held before the soul whether perceived, remembered, or anticipated, so that even thought ``never thinks without an image'' (\textit{DA} III.7, 431a16--17). The non-rational animal enters only through sense and \textit{phantasia}; the human may enter also through \textit{logos}-dependent thought---and it is this difference of entry, not any difference in the schema, that divides bare animal action from action proper to a rational being.

That all four causes meet at $A_4$---and that the three factors are themselves a partial reading of them---is no peculiarity of action but an instance of Aristotle's general doctrine of explanation, worth making explicit, for it yields the diagnostic instrument by which any completed action may be analyzed. To know a thing, Aristotle holds, is to know its cause, and \textit{cause} is said in four ways (\textit{Metaphysica} I.3, 983a24--32); a full account therefore answers four distinct questions---the four explanatory ``whys'' of the \textit{Physica}: out of what is the thing made (the material), in virtue of what is it this rather than that (the formal), what first set it going or altered it (the efficient), and that for the sake of which it comes to be (the final) (II.3, 194b24--195a3). These four converge in the single completed action---to diagnose it is to read them off that convergence. Yet none is exhausted there: each opens a series the act does not contain---the efficient running back through the movers that moved the mover, into the agent's sedimented history and the dispositional ground that disposes one to be moved as one is; the final running up through subordinate ends toward the good that orders the agent's conduct as a whole. To ask not merely what the four causes are but why \textit{this} agent was moved \textit{so} is to follow the efficient series back into disposition---the depth the diachronic loop, traced below, takes up.

The first two structures locate the source of an action without yet identifying it. The three-factor schema tells us \textit{that} the moved mover is desire---but desire (\textit{orexis}) is a genus, and it does not say which of its species moves in a given case; the four causes tell us \textit{that} the efficient cause is the \textit{archē}, the origin from which the motion springs---but ``\textit{archē}'' names a position to be filled, not the particular source that fills it. \textit{Which} desire, then, and \textit{what} origin? For human action the \textit{Rhetorica} answers, cataloguing the springs of conduct for the forensic orator, who must know what moves those to be prosecuted or defended. Aristotle sorts every action first by whether its origin lies in the agent at all:

\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
Now every action of every person either is or is not due to that person himself. Of those not due to himself some are due to chance, the others to necessity; of these latter, again, some are due to compulsion, the others to nature. Consequently all actions that are not due to a man himself are due either to chance or to nature or to compulsion. All actions that are due to a man himself and caused by himself are due either to habit or to desire; and of the latter, some are due to rational desire, the others to irrational. Rational desire is wishing, and wishing is a desire for good---nobody wishes for anything unless he thinks it good. Irrational desire is twofold, viz.\ anger and appetite. Thus every action must be due to one or other of seven causes: chance, nature, compulsion, habit, reasoning, anger, or appetite (Aristotle, \textit{Rhetorica}, I.10, 1368b33--1369a6).
\end{adjustwidth}

These seven are no second list beside the four causes but a specification of one of them---the efficient \textit{archē}, ``that which originates the movement'' (\textit{DA} III.10, 433b13--16)---sorted along two cuts. The first fixes voluntariness: chance, nature, and compulsion lodge the origin outside the agent and so are involuntary; habit, reasoning, anger, and appetite lodge it in the agent and so are the agent's own.\footnote{The involuntary three mark by default what voluntary action requires: chance has no determinate \textit{archē}; nature has an internal origin but one bypassing \textit{orexis} and deliberation (growth, the body's regular processes); compulsion is external force moving the agent against their own \textit{orexis}. This last must be kept distinct from the appetitive excess it resembles---the runaway appetite of the intemperate has its \textit{archē} \textit{within} the agent and remains their own, voluntary and answerable however much they disown it (\textit{Rhet}. I.11, 1370a6--9; \textit{NE} III.5, 1114a31--b3); the distinction bears weight when the diachronic loop is traced.} The next distinction divides the voluntary origins. Most source action through a species of \textit{orexis}, and since the completed action at $A_4$ is the actualization of a desire, the kind of action follows the kind of desire that drives it; these four types are taken up in the next subsection. One voluntary cause, however, sources action through no species of \textit{orexis} at all---habit, which moves through the familiarity that repetition lays down: ``acts are done from habit which men do because they have often done them before'' (\textit{Rhet}. I.10, 1369b6--7), and what is often done becomes, in effect, second nature---``as soon as a thing has become habitual, it is virtually natural'' (\textit{Rhet}. I.11, 1370a6--8). This habituated way of acting must not be conflated with the \textit{hexis} taken up below: habituation is the means by which a \textit{hexis} is cultivated, but the standing tendency to repeat what one has already done is not the settled dispositional ground it helps to form.

\subsubsection{The Four Voluntary Types of Action}

Of the seven causes, the desire-sourced voluntary origins are where an action first acquires its determinate kind. Since $A_4$ is the actualization of a desire, the species of desire that predominantly sources an action fixes its type. \textit{Orexis}, however, has but three species---\textit{epithymia}, \textit{thymos}, and \textit{boulēsis} (\textit{DA} II.3, 414b2)---so the types are not four coordinate kinds but three keyed to the species, with a fourth, the \textit{prohairetic}, that is no further species of desire but the deliberative completion of the rational wish.

\textbf{Epithymetic action} (\textit{epithymia}) is the most compressed configuration of the chain, the case in which ``the actualizing of desire is a substitute for inquiry or thinking'' (\textit{MA} 7, 701a29--33) and appetite issues in motion before any evaluatively complex \textit{doxa} can engage. It is the one type that can fire \textit{pre-doxastically}: the flinch from a blow, the recoil from what springs forward, moves on nothing more than the bare good-and-bad the \textit{kritikon} discriminates in the very act of perceiving (\textit{DA} III.7, 431a8--12). It need not be---judging another a pleasing companion and pursuing accordingly is epithymetic and \textit{doxastic} at once---but it is the only type that \textit{can} dispense with the \textit{doxa}; what individuates it is its source in appetite, not the level at which it operates.

\textbf{Thymotic action} (\textit{thymos}) answers a slight, defends honour, asserts the self---and always toward another. Anger arises by definition from a perceived slight, an appraisal that presupposes the agent's standing and the social matrix in which the slight registers as undeserved (\textit{Rhetorica} II.2, 1378a31--b5); \textit{thymos} is in this sense irreducibly social, its object disclosed within a \textit{toward-whom}, a Being-with (\textit{Mitsein}). Because it answers an appraisal, thymotic action is \textit{doxastic} throughout.

\textbf{Bouletic action} (\textit{boulēsis}) is sourced by the rational wish for an \textit{end}, and what marks it off is precisely that it is wish for the end and not yet choice of the means: ``we wish to be healthy,'' but ``we choose the acts which will make us healthy''; and because it reaches toward the end rather than instituting the realizable, \textit{boulēsis} may aim where choice cannot---there may be ``a wish even for impossibles, e.g.\ for immortality,'' or for what depends not on us but on others (\textit{NE} III.2, 1111b21--30) \textbf{[****PLACEHOLDER: NE/Ross(OWC); sub ROT****]}. Heidegger marks the same boundary: wishing, unlike resolute choice, ``can be directed at something that is impossible'' (\textit{BCAP} 99).

\textbf{Prohairetic action} (\textit{prohairesis}) is not a fourth species of desire but the \textit{boulēsis} carried to the realizable through deliberation about means---``having set the end, [we] consider how and by what means it is to be attained,'' and only over things ``in our power and can be done'' (\textit{NE} III.3, 1112a31--b19) \textbf{[****PLACEHOLDER: NE/Ross(OWC); sub ROT****]}. Where wish may reach the impossible, choice ``always goes after something that is under our control,'' carrying through to the \textit{éschaton}, the point ``that I genuinely institute through action'' (\textit{BCAP} 99). It is thus the completion, not the peer, of the bouletic, and in it deliberation completes itself \textit{as} an action; Aristotle's practical syllogism exhibits the form:

\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
But how is it that thought is sometimes followed by action, sometimes not; sometimes by movement, sometimes not? What happens seems parallel to the case of thinking and inferring about the immovable objects. There the end is the truth seen (for, when one thinks the two propositions, one thinks and puts together the conclusion), but here the two propositions result in a conclusion which is an action---for example, whenever one thinks that every man ought to walk, and that one is a man oneself, straightaway one walks; or that, in this case, no man should walk, one is a man: straightaway one remains at rest. And one so acts in the two cases provided that there is nothing to compel or to prevent (Aristotle, \textit{De Motu Animalium}, 701a7--16).
\end{adjustwidth}
The conclusion is no proposition but the deed itself---``straightaway one walks.'' Such resolution may also sediment into readiness, as the expert pianist realizes a passage through means long since chosen and now fluent.

These four are not exclusive compartments but name the desire that \textit{predominantly} sources an action; a single episode may pass through several, as pity may move first a bouletic wish to relieve a suffering and then a prohairetic choice of the means. And \textit{doxa} cross-cuts all four: any may be taken up under a taking-as-true, though the epithymetic alone can also fire beneath it.

That these are types at all must not be mistaken for a partition of the soul into separate desiring faculties, each discharging its own kind of deed. \textit{Aisthēsis}, \textit{phantasia}, and \textit{orexis} are one substrate differing only in being (\textit{DA} III.7, 431a13--14), and every type runs the one three-factor motion-structure through that single substrate; the species of desire is not a compartment the action issues from but the determination the one \textit{orexis} takes under a given disclosure. The types are accordingly not isolated powers but differentiated participations in a single structure of being-affected---modes of one being-in-the-world, not partitions within it. Read so, Aristotle's enumeration is preserved without the faculty-psychology a Heideggerian reading forecloses: the four articulate one desiring comportment, individuated by what predominantly moves it, not four powers lined up side by side.

Two further distinctions, however, cut across these four types, each independent of the type and of one another. The first is whether the end lies beyond the doing, in a product (\textit{poiēsis}), or is immanent in the doing itself (\textit{praxis})---the bivalence taken up below. The second is whether a \textit{doxa} ratifies the appearance at all, and, if it does, with what content. The voluntary/involuntary cut already drawn does not cross-cut these four, for all four are the agent's own; it is rather what sets them apart from the involuntary three. Knowing an action's type settles neither distinction: an action sprung from any of the four desires may be a making or a doing, ratified by \textit{doxa} or not. This independence is what keeps an intemperance from being mistaken for a compulsion. The addict's runaway appetite can look compelled, yet it is the agent's own through and through: voluntary in its origin, its \textit{archē} the agent's own appetite and no force from without; a \textit{praxis} in its terminus, a doing---if a degraded one; and the issue of an entrenched \textit{hexis}, a disposition the agent has laid down, not an external constraint. Compulsion would be the reverse---an origin outside the agent, overriding their own desire---which is just what the craving is not.

Any method of analysis, just like that which it proposes to analyze, will be best known through its end. As such, let us work through an example: We are hiking a hear a sound similar to a baby's rattle. We stop, trying to make out what it is and where it came from; we hear it again, and we take the sound \textit{as} a rattlesnake---though we see no snake, no creature at all---and, hoping not to provoke what we have not seen, we back slowly away and return as we came. To diagnose the retreat is to answer the four whys at once. Its \textit{matter} is the body moved, with the bodily alteration the fear itself is---the quickened pulse, the caught breath, the muscles gathering to withdraw (\textit{MA}, 7, 701b16--32). Its \textit{form} is the retreat \textit{as} retreat, the \textit{eidos} of the motion, an avoidance of the apparent destructive evil; what makes the motion \textit{this} withdrawal and not an advance or a standing-firm is the species of desire that sources it (fear) together with its object, the rattlesnake-taken-as-impending-harm. Here the formal determination is supplied wholly by \textit{phantasia}'s \textit{taking-as}, for nothing is given to sight: the sound is present, but the snake and its threat are present only as \textit{phantasia} renders them. Its \textit{efficient} cause is the \textit{orektikon} in act---the desiring part, moved by its object and moving the body through the organic parts---and behind the desire, as the standing disposition from which this very taking-of-the-sound-as-fearful proceeds, the agent's \textit{hexis}, the cultivated \textit{how} from which the coward's flight and the courageous person's holding-firm alike take their orientation. Its \textit{end} is the escape from the impending evil, the safety for the sake of which the whole motion is set going. Set beside the three-factor schema, these are not four findings laid side by side but the schema seen from the side of explanation: the object of desire is at once unmoved originator and final cause---safety as the good to attain, the rattlesnake as the evil to shun---the fear-specified \textit{orexis} is the moved mover and efficient cause, the body is what is moved and the matter in which the motion is realized, and the formal cause is read off their convergence, the determinate evil the appearance presents.

In the order of experience the motion runs forward---the object strikes sense, sense and \textit{phantasia} actualize, desire is moved, the body moves, the end is attained; but in the order of analysis we must begin where execution ends, with the completed act, and recover its principles in reverse. Aristotle warrants the inversion twice: the object defines the act and the act the faculty, so that activities are prior in account to the potentialities they actualize, and prior to both stand their correlative objects (\textit{DA} II.4, 415a16--22); and, since the actuality is the end, the potency takes its very name from the act it issues in (\textit{Met}. IX.8, 1050a21--25). The completed act is what is first \textit{for us}---given, evident, the thing to be explained---while the object and end it realizes are first \textit{in nature}; analysis is the passage from the one to the other (\textit{Phys}. I.1, 184a16--23). So we begin by fixing the \textit{energeia}, the retreat accomplished: this names the \textit{explanandum} and, in the same stroke, the realized \textit{telos}, since the completed act \textit{is} its own end.

From that terminus the three structures unfold together, each supplying what the others cannot. The three-factor schema is already a partial four-cause analysis of self-movement: the object of desire, as unmoved originator, is the final cause; the \textit{orektikon} in act, as moved mover, is the efficient cause---the fear that, moved by its object, moves the body; and the animal moved is the matter in which the motion is realized. Three of the four causes thus lie within the three factors; the formal cause alone stands outside them, read off their convergence as the \textit{eidos} of the motion. What the three factors leave open is \textit{which} source occupies the moved mover's place, and this only the seven rhetorical causes decide: they individuate the \textit{archē} and fix its voluntariness. Where the agent is the \textit{archē}, the source is one of the three desires---\textit{boulēsis}, \textit{thymos}, \textit{epithymia}---that may fill the moved mover's place, with habit as the dispositional ground beneath them Where the \textit{archē} lies otherwise, the source is nature working as an internal formal-final principle, or chance, or compulsion; and, only once the source is fixed does the form complete itself: the generic \textit{eidos}, avoidance, was legible from the object-as-evil at the first step, but whether the flight is a reflexive bolt, a \textit{thymotic} recoil, or a calculated retreat does not crystallize until the source is named. The form is doubly determined, its second determination downstream of the seven-cause individuation.

This is why the same end or \textit{telos}, escape or flight in this case, may spring from different sources. The flight of bare animal action and the flight of deliberated fear both make for safety, but they are not one motion. When the evil is \textit{in the percept}---the rattle sounding, present and already aversive---the \textit{phantasma} is aversive from the first, and the flight can discharge at once, no \textit{doxa} required: this is the floor of action the perceiving animal shares. Where the evil is \textit{not} in the percept but must be read from a sign---Aristotle's beacon taken as the mark of an advancing enemy, the destructive evil still wholly to come---nothing aversive is yet present to be fled, and the motion cannot fire until \textit{doxa} ratifies the inference. The rattlesnake heard but unseen falls between: the sound is present and aversive, enough to move even an animal, yet to take it \textit{as} a rattlesnake, and the rattlesnake as deadly and impending, is already to have crossed into the \textit{taking-as-true} that only \textit{doxa} affords. The threshold is just here---where \textit{phantasia}'s bare aversive tone passes into the evaluatively complex \textit{doxa} that fear proper requires---and it is the threshold an ethical \textit{hexis} commands: the hiker, fear answering the sound, retreats; the naturalist, the same fear answering the same sound but met by a cultivated courage, holds, listens again, and moves to identify what sounded. One \textit{phantasma}, one \textit{doxa} of danger, two shapes of action, divided by the \textit{hexis} that orients the fear.

%---END ThreeStructures---

%---BEGIN 06-HexisBivalence (consolidated single arc 2026-06-18; target ~1,300w)---
\subsubsection{\textit{Hexis} Bivalence: \textit{Technē} and Ethical Hexis}

\textit{Hexis} is the settled, durable disposition from which an agent's \textit{doxai} and actions proceed---``the having \ldots\ a kind of activity of the haver and the had, something like an action or movement'' (\textit{Met}. V.20, 1022b5)\footnote{The original Greek translated as ``having'' and ``activity'' is, respectively, \gk{Ἕξις} (\textit{hexis}) and \gk{ἐνέργειά} (\textit{energeia}); Greek text in W. D. Ross, ed., \textit{Aristotle's Metaphysics}, vol.~1 (Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1924).}---marked by its resistance to easy displacement, so that knowledge and moral excellence count as paradigmatic \textit{hexeis} while passing moods and fleeting opinions do not (\textit{Cat}. 8, 8b25--9a13). It is not itself a \textit{doxa}: a \textit{doxa} is a \textit{taking-as-true}, and the universal premises an agent draws on in acting---``every man ought to walk,'' and the like---are \textit{held doxai} a \textit{hexis} may incline one to hold, not \textit{hexeis} themselves (\textit{MA} 7, 701a7--16); nor is it the habit that cultivates it, for habituation forms a \textit{hexis} without being the disposition it forms. Heidegger's recovery of the term fixes what a \textit{hexis} is with precision: it is ``nothing other than a how of \textit{pathos}, being-out-of-composure, in relation to being-composed-as-to ...'' (\textit{BCAP} 125)---not a cognitive deposit but the dispositional form, the cultivated \textit{how} of standing in an emotion, as courage is to fear. The dispositional ground does not operate in a single register: the same genus that, as ethical \textit{hexis}, governs the \textit{praxis} whose end is immanent also appears as the \textit{hexis} of \textit{technē} that governs the \textit{poiēsis} whose end is a product beyond it. It is bivalent, and Heidegger's 1924 reading of the \textit{Rhetorica} draws the two poles apart.

The first pole is the \textit{hexis} of \textit{technē}---and the designation is Aristotle's own: \textit{technē}, he says, is ``identical with a state of capacity to make, involving a true course of reasoning'' (\textit{NE} VI.4, 1140a9--10), where the ``state'' is the \textit{hexis} and the ``capacity to make'' (\textit{poiētikē}) marks it as the \textit{hexis} of \textit{poiēsis}.\footnote{Greek: \gk{ταὐτὸν ἂν εἴη τέχνη καὶ ἕξις μετὰ λόγου ἀληθοῦς ποιητική} (\textit{tautòn àn eíē téchnē kaì héxis metà lógou alēthoûs poiētikḗ}); text from the Oxford Classical Text, \textit{Aristotelis Ethica Nicomachea}, ed. I. Bywater (Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1962). The genus is \gk{ἕξις} (\textit{hexis}); its differentia \gk{ποιητική} (\textit{poiētikḗ}, ``of making'') sets it against the \gk{πρακτική} (\textit{praktikḗ}, ``of acting'') by which \textit{phronēsis} is defined at \textit{NE} VI.5, 1140b4--5.} The being of a \textit{technē} depends on the \textit{telos} as the disposition cultivated through training in a productive art, where the \textit{ergon}---the work, the product---is decisive: ``[s]hoemaking depends upon the shoe ... being a good, suitable shoe'' (\textit{BCAP} 123). Its structural logic is the reduction of deliberation. Heidegger states:

\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
Through practice, by frequently-undergoing, it comes about that being-oriented puts the prescription further and further out of play. Training has the precise sense of reducing deliberation insofar as it is through training that the completedness of attaining a result comes about. (\textit{BCAP} 127)
\end{adjustwidth}

The trained craft fires as routine---the grammarian writes ``not by chance, but according to a prescription ... from out of himself'' (\textit{BCAP} 127)---and its structural analogue at the existential level is the ready-to-hand equipment that withdraws from explicit attention into the work it serves: the hammer in the master carpenter's grip disappears into the hammering itself, becoming thematic only when it breaks, goes missing, or stands in the way (\textit{Being and Time} 98).\footnote{As Heidegger argues regarding the readiness-to-hand of equipment in \textit{Being and Time} : ``The hammering itself uncovers the specific `manipulability' [``Handlichkeit''] of the hammer. The kind of Being which equipment possesses---in which it manifests itself in its own right---we call ``\textit{readiness-to-hand}'' [\textit{Zuhandenheit}]. Only because equipment has \textit{this} `Being-in-itself' and does not merely occur, is it manipulable in the broadest sense and at our disposal. No matter how sharply we just \textit{look} [Nur-noch-\textit{hinsehen}] at the `outward appearance' [``Aussehen]'' of Things in whatever form this takes, we cannot discover anything ready-to-hand. If we look at Things just `theoretically', we can get along without understanding readiness-to-hand. But when we deal with them by using them and manipulating them, this activity is not a blind one; it has its own kind of sight by which our manipulation is guided and from which it acquires its specific Thingly character. Dealings with equipment subordinate themselves to the manifold assignments of the `in-order-to'. And the sight with which they thus accommodate themselves is \textit{circumspection}'' (98). The structural homology with \textit{technē} is precise: trained craft-knowledge operates transparently while the work proceeds, becoming thematic only when the equipment fails or something interferes with the process.} The \textit{technē}, like the hammer, withdraws while it operates. Its chain-architectural signature is compression. A cultivated \textit{technē} adds no stage to the actualization chain but compresses its passage through one: the deliberative arc at $A_3$ collapses into a transparent procedural recognition, so that the chain runs from $A_2$ through $A_3$ to $A_4$ as if at a single stroke. The \textit{doxa} is not thereby bypassed---no stage drops out---but passed through transparently: what operates at $A_3$ keeps the \textit{doxatic} form of a pre-thematic ``this is how it is done,'' a craft-directive relied upon rather than weighed. It remains a \textit{doxa}---so \textit{technē} stays a rational disposition, its \textit{logos} retained but rendered transparent---yet a \textit{doxa} of a peculiar authority. Where an ordinary \textit{doxa} is held on the standing of those who hold it, the artisan's recognition answers to the \textit{eidos} of the product: the shoe meets the form of a good shoe or it does not, an \textit{archē} that is not merely social.\footnote{That the mature \textit{technē} answers to the \textit{eidos} does not free it of \textit{doxa} at the root. The artisan's procedural recognition is drawn from a standing fore-having (\textit{Vorhabe})---the already-interpreted craft-world into which the apprentice grows, what counts as good leather or a well-made shoe---received first on the authority of masters and the craft tradition, that is, as \textit{doxa} in the ordinary social sense. The \textit{eidos} that later anchors the \textit{technē} independently is thus itself first given \textit{doxatically}; repeated production refines this inherited view into a reliable knowing-one's-way-around, anchored in the work's success or failure, without ever fully evacuating the \textit{doxatic} structure from the craft's operational floor.} And just because the appearance is taken under this aspect---as the means to a determinate \textit{ergon}, not under the evaluatively charged aspect that would summon an emotion---the higher-order evaluative axis remains unengaged so long as the craft runs transparently. The deliberative passage to action is itself a motion, ``a kind of activity---an imperfect kind'' (\textit{DA} II.5, 417a16), which \textit{technē} does not carry through but abbreviates. This compression is defeasible, however: let the work resist---the tool breaks, the material refuses to comply, the case falls outside the routine---and $A_3$ re-thickens, deliberation and the evaluative axis alike returning with the situation now to be weighed, just as the hammer that had withdrawn into the hammering phenomenologically reappears the moment it breaks in the hand.

\inlinenote{Make sure to come back to this in the next part discussing repeating tactics for internalizing mechanics of playing, the goal of which is to ``reduce deliberation'' through training. }

The second pole is the ethical \textit{hexis}, and its structure is the contrary of routine. The being of the human being, unlike the being of the shoe, ``is determined in its \gk{ἔργον} [\textit{ergon}] as \gk{πρᾶξις} [\textit{praxis}] ... [which] has the \gk{τέλος} [\textit{telos}] in itself \ldots\ It depends on \gk{ἕξις} [\textit{hexis}]'' (\textit{BCAP} 123). What such a \textit{hexis} cultivates is not a routine but a readiness:

\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
Cultivating \gk{ἕξις} [\textit{hexis}] never depends on an operation, a routine. In an operation, the moment is destroyed. Every completedness, as settled routine, breaks down in the face of the moment. Appropriation and cultivation of \gk{ἕξις} through habituation means nothing other than correct repetition.... The distinction lies in the fact that \gk{πρᾶξις} [\textit{praxis}] depends on the \textit{how}. The how is only appropriated in such a way that the human being enables himself \textit{to be composed at each moment}; not routine but holding-oneself-open, \gk{δύναμις} [\textit{dynamis}] in the \gk{μεσότης} [\textit{mesōtes}]. (\textit{BCAP} 128)
\end{adjustwidth}
Where the \textit{technē} contracts deliberation toward transparent routine, the ethical \textit{hexis} sustains the standing capacity for fresh resolution---the \textit{prohairesis}, the deliberate choice of what to do---in the face of the \textit{kairos}. It does not withdraw into transparency but holds the agent open for renewed \textit{prohairetic} engagement on each occasion; and Heidegger is explicit that no \textit{technē} can stand in for it: ``this determination should not be conceived as though there were a \gk{τέχνη} [\textit{technē}] for this taking-opportunities and venturing-out into the \gk{δεινά} [\textit{deina}] of life'' (\textit{BCAP} 122--123). The \textit{kairos} is precisely what routine forecloses. The canonical practical-rational \textit{hexis} of this kind is \textit{phronēsis}, ``a true and reasoned state of capacity to act with regard to the things that are good or bad for man,'' which Aristotle marks off from \textit{technē} on just this ground---that \textit{praxis} has no external \textit{ergon} but is its own \textit{telos} (\textit{NE} VI.5, 1140b4--5).\footnote{For Aristotle's full discussion of \textit{phronēsis} or practical wisdom, see \textit{NE} VI.5, a24--b30.}

These are therefore not two species of one function but structurally distinct modes of being-composed, and their difference tells directly in the chain. A \textit{hexis} is not a \textit{doxa}, yet it conditions \textit{doxa}-formation: it enters the motion from \textit{phantasma} to \textit{doxa} ($M_2 \rightarrow A_3$) as a relative unmoved originator alongside the \textit{phantasma} itself, helping determine what the agent takes the appearance to be---for ``the end appears to each man in a form answering to his character,'' so that one is in part answerable for how things appear (\textit{NE} III.5, 1114a31--b3).\footnote{``Now some one may say that all men aim at the apparent good, but have no control over how things appear to him; but the end appears to each man in a form answering to his character. We reply that if each man is somehow responsible for the state he is in, he will also be himself somehow responsible for how things appear'' (\textit{NE} III.5, 1114a31--b3).} The two poles condition it differently. Where the \textit{technē} contracts the chain to a transparent routine, the ethical \textit{hexis} exercises a two-level reach, working beneath the \textit{doxa}, on the hedonic tone of perception, and at the \textit{doxa} itself. The lower reach is the harder to see. Beneath any \textit{taking-as-true}, the \textit{hexis} inflects the basic hedonic tonality with which perception completes itself at $A_1$, and the mechanism is internal to what a \textit{hexis} is. The hedonic tonality at $A_1$ is nothing other than the discriminating dimension of \textit{aisthēsis} disclosing a present object as attractive or aversive in the very act of perceiving it; and a \textit{hexis} is the cultivated \textit{how} of being-affected (\textit{paschein}), the settled tuning of that very susceptibility. So the disposition inflects the tone in which the object is disclosed---tilting one and the same occasion to register as more aversive to a timorous soul, less so to a courageous one---and it does so before any \textit{doxa} is formed: the perceptual field arrives already tonally pitched, not a neutral given awaiting judgement. At the upper level the \textit{hexis} then conditions which \textit{taking-as-true} the \textit{phantasma} settles into at $A_3$---and here the agent is not the author of the \textit{doxa} but its subject, for a \textit{doxa} is an involuntary \textit{taking-as-true}, a being-convinced one undergoes rather than an endorsement one issues. The \textit{hexis} does not let the agent affirm a \textit{doxa} at will but inclines which one takes hold, the appearance settling into the construal that answers to the agent's character. The two levels are continuous, the lower already half the work of the upper: an appearance that arrives aversively pitched is the readier to settle into a \textit{doxa} of danger---which is how the \textit{hexis} shapes the \textit{doxa} without the agent ever electing it, and so conditions which determinate emotion issues.
